‘How Are You Supposed to Have Sex with a Wicker Dick? I Wanted to See’

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

When indie film distributor Black Bear released the first trailer for Wicker, it hit social media timelines with a bang. Based on the short story The Wicker Husband by Ursula Wills-Jones, the film follows a small village fisherwoman (Olivia Colman) who convinces a local basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to build her a husband out of wicker (Alexander Skarsgård). Fans, naturally, went a bit feral over the scenes of Colman and a basket-ified Skarsgård consummating their marriage so rapturously they break the bed. Eventually, a common joke emerged: “Will this be available in 4DX?”

Sometimes, for the pure of heart, prayers are answered. On Oct. 28, Black Bear put Wicker in 4DX for a one-night-only event, allowing audiences to experience the low-fantasy romance complete with water, wind, snow, and all the thrusting their hearts desired. But while the idea initially stemmed from a joke, the demand was real. Tickets sold so fast that theaters extended showings until Nov. 5. And the romance fans who threw their cards down first tell Rolling Stone that what they thought might be an absurd night out turned into a raucous, communal experience — one they say highlights what audiences might want more of out of romance movies.

If you ask New York City resident Robert Belpasso, 32, he probably should’ve realized how serious people were about seeing the film in 4DX when he tried to buy tickets. Showings would populate and then sell out as he was trying to click purchase. “Initially, it was impossible to get tickets,” he says. “It was like fighting like a Ticketmaster war.”

Another New Yorker, Leen Dweik, and her friends didn’t even blink when it looked like the only available seats were at a theater in suburban New Rochelle — a 30- to 45-minute trip outside of the city. Luckily, seats opened up at a Regal in Times Square, but it really didn’t matter. They had no plans to miss it.

“I’ve never seen anything in 4DX before — I’m not necessarily interested in having things sprayed at me,” Dweik, 29, says. “But it did feel like if there was ever a film to try it for, I wanted to try it for this one. It was so clear that everybody who was there really wanted to be there. Some people even made merch.”  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Tia Burkett, 33, gathered a group of friends in Kansas to see Wicker in 4DX after watching the trailer online. But she was surprised by how earnest the story was — and how aggressively her seat moved during certain scenes. “There was one point where I was like, ‘Oh, my gosh, I’m about to fall.’ That first sex scene truly felt like we were on a roller coaster and none of us were expecting it. I’ve never laughed that hard in my life,” she says. “The whole ‘getting wicked down’ thing was just something I wanted to be a part of.”

There’s an obvious titilatting element to the film that’s drawing an audience in via social media — the film’s marketing has drilled down on the idea that this movie is about a woman fucking her wicker husband. The film delights in its arborial sound effects, twigs cracking and limbs growing alongside other wood-like appendages. Who wouldn’t be interested to learn more?

Karen Turner, 36, already says she’ll see anything Olivia Colman is in. But the technical elements of the story did convince her to buy a 4DX ticket to Regal’s L.A. Live theater. “It’s utterly ridiculous,” she says. “How are you supposed to have sex with a wicker dick? That doesn’t make sense anatomically. So I had questions going in. I wanted to see.”

She’s not the only one who felt compelled to investigate. “The funniest thing that I saw the entire time was everybody trying to figure out if there was going to be ‘full wick’ or not,” says Molly S., 32,  who saw the film with Belpasso. “It started hitting the rumor mills that the European version isn’t censored, but the U.S. version is. Everyone went in [to] do boots-on-the-ground journalism. That was just hysterical.”  Related Content Alexander Skarsgård Embraces His Magnetism in the Rolling Stones’ Video for ‘Mr. Charm’ Alexander Skarsgard’s Queer-Biker BDSM Rom-Com Is Definitely NSFW ‘This Is Spinal Brat’ — Charli XCX Unleashes ‘The Moment’ at Sundance Charli XCX’s ‘The Moment’ Is Fiction, But Also ‘The Realest Depiction of the Music Industry’ She’s Seen

“And there was full-frontal wick,” Belpasso confirms.

“Very tiny — from far away,” Molly clarifies. “Well,” Belpasso adds. “Quite large.”

Post Covid-19 pandemic, audiences have returned to cinemas across the country, including a dedicated surge in 4DX interest. Still, the format typically only offers action films, banking on fans wanting to experience the swerves and jumps from battles, high-impact car chases, or daring stunts. Wicker’s earnings at the box office so far aren’t huge — the film has currently grossed $13 million globally on an initial $10 million production budget — which makes it a moderate success for its independent status. But the cult status of the film online, which has only grown since its 4DX foray, indicates there might be an audience demand that theaters — and filmmakers — have underestimated.

Gracie D. has believed for years that traditional Hollywood romance releases have had a chemistry problem that could be solved with some creative shakeups. The 26-year-old considers herself “very invested” in romances like Wicker that center sentient objects. “I’ve been reading the genre for years. It’s not new. There are books about sentient doors and lamps. I read one about a polyamorous romance between a woman and a pair of Crocs,” she says. “The big appeal for Wicker is that it’s silly and it doesn’t take itself 100 percent seriously.”

With romantasy — books that combine romance and fantasy elements like magic — is a top-selling book genre, it’s not surprising that fandom translated to box office results for Wicker. But while the film’s 4DX demand is proof that sex still sells, the movie’s deeper strength lies in its use of Skarsgård and Colman’s sex life to slyly critique the town’s assumptions about female desire and agency — something many of the moviegoers who spoke to Rolling Stone said they appreciated above all else.  Trending Stories Trump's Bizarre Journey Through the Stages of Grief as Democrats Dominated the Midterms As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000 Bob Dylan Meets With Pope Leo XIV in a Private Audience Dane Cook’s Brother Robbed Him Blind. Now He’s Rebuilding His Life — And Gunning for a Comeback

“In romance films recently, Hollywood [is] throwing two hot people at the screen and expecting you to believe it when they take their clothes off,” Gracie says. “Wicker is presenting itself initially as something horny and weird, but what makes it so enjoyable and popular is that it also has these themes of women wanting to be respected in their partnerships.”

“I think that there’s something really profound in the explorations of romance between woman and other,” Dweik adds. “And from the get-go, Wicker was committing to the concept of being freaky and horny. People just want more freak shit.”

PSV edged out by sharper Club Brugge in Champions League defeat

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

PSV Eindhoven endured a frustrating night in the Champions League as their defensive frailties were exposed in a 2-1 defeat by Club Brugge.

PSV started full of attacking intent and should have taken an early lead when Ivan Perisic put Sergiño Dest clean through on goal, but the defender’s shot was too close to goalkeeper Yann Sommer.

Instead it was the visitors who took the lead with their first real attack when Nicolo Tresoldi headed in a teasing cross from Joaquin Seys.

PSV equalised early in the second half as an outstanding pass by Sven Mijnans into the path Lutsharel Geertruida allowed the defender to charge to the byline. His pullback found its way to Kodai Sano, whose shot took a deflection off Seys into the net.

But five minutes later PSV were caught out as another razor-sharp pass by Seys released Carlos Forbs in space to net the winner.

The home side refused to give up, but were unable to find a way past Sommer in the closing stages as the Belgian side closed out the win.

Team captain Mauro Junior admitted that PSV had been undone by shortcomings at the back. “We had a lot of opportunities in the first half but we didn’t score,” he told Ziggo Sport.

“We want to attack and score goals, but we weren’ well organised today in defence. We need to keep our heads up and look forward to the next game.”

The result leaves PSV in 24th place, just above the elimination line, ahead of their next match away to Real Madrid on November 24, with four points from their opening four matches.

With just about everything getting more expensive, luckily, some grocery stores are offering Thanksgiving meal deals that can help cut costs while making your holiday cooking a whole lot easier.

But with so many options, how do you know which one gives you the most bang for your buck? I looked through the latest meal deals to find out where you can get the most food for the least money.

Viktor Gyokeres stars for Arsenal in slim Champions League win over Slavia Prague

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Slavia Prague 1-2 Arsenal: The Swedish striker provided Noni Madueke with an assist and then scored a stylish goal as the Gunners continued their impressive form

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Viktor Gyokeres grasped his opportunity as Arsenal beat Slavia Prague 2-1 in the Champions League.

Gyokeres made the most of a rare start, his sixth of the season, with an assist and a stylish goal as the Gunners climbed to third in the Champions League table.

Noni Madueke scored the opener and Arsenal were cruising until the closing stages, when Wiktor Nowak pulled one back to leave Mikel Arteta’s men hanging on in stoppage time.

Gyokeres, whose only previous goal this season was in the Carabao Cup defeat at Fleetwood, was one of six changes Arteta made to the side which beat Liverpool 1-0 at the weekend.

Ben White and Piero Hincapie started in defence in the Czech capital with Martin Zubimendi and Mikel Merino slotting into midfield and Eberechi Eze a lively presence behind Gyokeres.

There was also a late run-out for youngster Max Dowman, who is suspended domestically but eligible for Europe following his red card at Fleetwood.

Madueke put them ahead in the ninth minute after Gyokeres muscled his marker off the ball on the halfway line and hooked the ball forward into space.

The England winger raced past the defence and slid his angled shot through the legs of Nazar Domchak.

The Slavia goalkeeper then had to come out and block from Gyokeres, tip Merino’s drive over the crossbar and make a fine reflex save from the Spain international.

Arsenal had a mild scare eight minutes before half-time when Tomas Holes got free in the area at a long throw and poked his shot wide.

But the Premier League champions immediately went up the other end and doubled their lead through Gyokeres.

The Sweden striker exchanged passes with Merino as he made his way into the area before opening up his body and clipping the ball across Domchak and in off the far post.

It was the 28-year-old’s first Champions League goal from open play since he scored against Kairat Almaty in January.

Arsenal were almost caught cold seconds after the break when Oskar Kubiak got forward and forced David Raya into his first save of the match.

Madueke almost had a second after more good play from Gyokeres, who held up Declan Rice’s pass before laying the ball off to Christos Tzolis.

Fit-again Tzolis fed Madueke out on the right and his fierce angled drive was blocked by the chest of Domchak.

Arsenal retreated for the remainder of the match and Gabriel had to clear a header from Slavia substitute Ange N’Guessan off the goal-line.

The VAR checked for a potential penalty after Merino knocked over Nowak as they challenged for the rebound, but it was deemed an accidental clash of heads.

In an unneccesarily nervous ending Raya had to make a full-stretch save to push Kubiak’s header against a post.

And the Gunners lost their clean sheet when Nowak converted David Jurasek’s cross for a deserved consolation for the hosts.

The World’s Most Famous Architects Are Building in Albania. I Had to Find Out Why

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“I'll try to focus on this, which is not the easiest thing today,” says Edi Rama, the embattled prime minister of Albania, after a night spent monitoring violent protests in the capital, Tirana. But Rama quickly warms to one of his favorite subjects. “Architecture,” he says, choosing his words carefully, “has the power to change societies. It’s like a strong medicine. It can heal, or it can kill.”

A journalist who covers architecture, I had been hearing about a building boom in Albania for several years. It seemed like half the architects I knew in New York were on a first-name basis with the prime minister, a working artist and, at 6’-7”, a former pro basketball player. So on October 19 I flew to Tirana—a short side-trip from Greece, where I was on vacation with my husband—to look at some of the new buildings and interview Rama about them. But I arrived during a season of civil unrest. The prime minister, whose assistant had told I needn’t postpone the trip because everything was fine, had moved to a secure location on the outskirts of the city; some of the buildings I had come to see had just been vandalized by protesters; and at one point I was accused, publicly, of aiding a government spy. (The charge was— need I even say this—absurd.) And, because I’m in the early stages of Parkinson’s disease, I experienced all this from a four-wheeled mobility scooter, which I had rented for about $100.

As I tooled around the city center, much of it seemed unaffected by the protests. In fact, most of Tirana was charming. But weren’t those Molotov cocktails we heard going off during an al fresco dinner? Those weren’t on my drinks menu.

What was on my menu in Tirana, a city of about 600,000 in a country of about 2.3 million, was confusion. Confusion about why the protesters were so angry, and about why the prime minister seemed so unconcerned. In his 13 years in office, Rama, 62, has been remaking as much of Albania as possible with the help of foreign architects. Even the heavily guarded villa where he agreed to be interviewed, a raw concrete building dyed bright red, is largely the work of the Spanish architect Pablo Bofill.

When protesters began demanding Rama’s resignation last May, he was about to host his second annual gathering of architects from around the world, one of the high points of his year. Architects like the Danish superstar Bjarke Ingels and the Chilean Alejandro Aravena, who won the Pritzker Prize in 2016, “pay to come here and talk and they love it,” Rama says. But this year the get-together, from June 3 to 5, drew protesters with signs that read “Starchitects go home!” Those protests presaged the more violent demonstrations in October and November.

I haven’t attended either of Rama’s conclaves, but what I’ve heard reminds me of my visit to Ordos, Inner Mongolia, in 2008, which I wrote about for The New York Times. A wealthy industrialist, aided by the artist Ai Weiwei, had brought 100 young architects from around the world to the isolated desert city. Their task was to design 100 villas for anonymous (and seemingly nonexistent) Chinese millionaires. The architects picked numbers out of a bowl to determine which of the 100 sites was theirs, and before flying home they were paid for their designs with wads of cash. None of the villas was built, but being in Ordos was a bonding experience for a generation of architects, a few of whom have recently been to Tirana. At least until the latest protests, the mood among Rama-anointed architects—giddy optimism coupled with uncertainty about what would actually be built—echoed the mood in Ordos almost 20 years ago. Both situations felt surreal.

Rama normally works in an office on the Boulevard of Martyrs of the Nation in the center of Tirana. But on October 20 the boulevard's sidewalks were missing hundreds of paving stones, which protesters had used to shatter storefronts. (Some that weren’t shattered bore hastily made signs telling protesters, “Don’t target us. We are with you.”) And some of the stones were reportedly thrown at police during nighttime clashes that authorities say left hundreds injured. Police have used tear gas and rubber bullets as protesters set small buildings, like an information kiosk in the vast Skanderbeg Square, on fire. Near the shell of the kiosk, workers scrubbed fresh graffiti off a statue of Skanderbeg himself.

The Tirana Pyramid, built by followers of the brutal dictator Enver Hoxha, who died in 1985, as his memorial, and turned into a public amenity by the Rotterdam firm MVRDV, was also damaged.

There’s a reason some Albanians—it’s impossible to say how many—resent the foreign architects and the attention lavished on them. In the 26 years since he became the mayor of Tirana and then prime minister, Rama has initiated or encouraged more than 50 international architecture competitions for projects ranging from parks to museums to large housing developments. Forty-eight of those were won by foreign architects (some working with Albanian firms), according to Adelina Greca, who leads the National Territorial Planning Agency, which organizes competitions for the government. Only two were won by Albanian firms working alone. In addition, Rama has played matchmaker, giving private developers the names of foreign architects he strongly recommends they hire.

As a result, Tirana’s compact city center is filled with buildings in bright colors and novel shapes. There is a so-called vertical forest, a skyscraper laden with trees and shrubs, by the Italian architect Stefano Boeri. The green-colored building is just a stone’s throw from the bright red Marriott by another Italian architect, Marco Casamonti. The Plaza Hotel, by architect Johan Anrys, of the Belgian firm 51N4E, starts as an ellipse at its base and morphs into a rectangle at its crown. But the champion attention-getter is a condo building by MVRDV in the shape of a head. Its facade and balconies resolve into facial features, most conspicuously a pronounced nose. The architects have publicly identified it as the head of Skanderbeg, a 15th century military hero. But some Albanians think it’s Rama. Both ideas are “urban legends,” says the prime minister. “It’s the form of a man through history,” he says, contradicting the architects’ own statement. Nodding at this reporter, he says, “It also looks like you."

Tirana's Rock (aka the Skandebeg building) is an MVRDV-designed condo in the shape of a head

A Marriott designed by Marco Casamonti of Archea Associati, left, and the “vertical forest” skyscraper by Stefano Boeri

The vertical forest, the Marriott and the Plaza Hotel are all handsome additions to the city. So, too, is the “head,” but is it too much of an architectural one-liner? Who, I wonder as I pass it several times a day, is going to want to live in the nose?

Rama tells me—on-the-record and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.”

Anrys, who 22 years ago became one of the first foreign architects in Albania, told me that working there has taught him "what architecture truly means for improving society.” He said he knows that Albanian architects wonder, "Are we not good enough?" and believe we're taking jobs from them. "It isn't true. I have a studio in Tirana with 20 Albanian architects." Overall he says, the foreign architects have “brought Albanian architects more work than we've taken."

Rama tells me—on-the-record and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.” The rest, he explains, see that having first-rate architects from abroad, including Herzog & de Meuron, OMA, and Diller Scofidio & Renfro, working in Albania will raise everyone’s game.

Rama tells me—on-the-record and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.”

The foreign architects who win competitions are required to enlist architects licensed in Albania to help them execute their plans. Rama compares the situation to that of China, which 20 years ago was giving its most prominent building commissions to foreign architects, including some of the same ones working in Albania today. Chinese architects, Rama says, got a free education from the western masters.

“If we continue this kind of cooperation,” says Greca, who has worked for Rama almost continuously since 2003, “there will come a day when Albanian architects will win competitions not only here but around the world.”

Anti-government protesters on the streets of Tirana, with the art installation Tirana Moons by Austrian architect Chris Precht in the background

But Greca’s association with Rama has made her a controversial figure among local architects. On my first day in town, our route intersects that of a lunchtime protest march. We stop to watch. Greca notices many architects and architecture students in the crowd. (The Faculty of Architecture and Urbanism of the Polytechnic University of Tirana is nearby.)

Within minutes, Gent Progni, an organizer of the march, posts a photo of Greca and me on Instagram. The caption claims she had sunk so low as to use a disabled person (me) as cover to spy on the marchers. (Ten days later, the post has almost 7,000 “likes.”) Later, in a text responding to questions from this reporter, Progni wrote:

“My opposition is not to foreign architects but to the way Edi Rama uses prestigious international names to legitimize controversial development projects, often without transparency, meaningful public consultation, or accountability. This is a significant issue for our protest movement, but it is part of a much larger struggle against corruption, the concentration of power, and the sale of Albania’s public wealth.”

Rama himself isn’t happy with all of the new buildings in Tirana. He complained about one whose facades are made of stone “until a certain altitude,” where the cost-cutting developer switched to a printed material that only looks like stone. Other buildings stand partially complete, or complete but eerily empty. And in several cases developers have replaced foreign architects with local ones midstream, in order to complete buildings without meeting the original design specifications. “We need the investors to stick with the architects,” Rama says. But funding is less available than he would like. “Certain architecture needs a certain quality,” he says, philosophically. “But compromises are forced by the limitations of the place.”

The problems with his building boom go deeper than facade materials. There are accusations of money laundering and other forms of corruption, which grew louder at the end of May, when Rama agreed to support efforts by a company associated with Jared Kushner and Ivanka Trump to build luxury resorts on previously undeveloped land. It was a coincidence that the big architecture conference started just a few days later, leading directly to the calls for starchitects to scram. Among the protesters, that demand became entangled with environmental and anti-Trump sentiments in what became known as the Flamingo Revolution.

A housing project by Madrid-based firm SelgasCano

European journalist and curator Anneke Abhelakh has recently published a brilliantly illuminating book, The Albanian Files, which gives 60 foreign architecture firms eight or more pages to describe the work they are doing in Albania. Altogether, the book lists more than 500 projects. Of those, only about one-tenth have been realized. Rama says he is looking forward to the completion of many more.

But can that really happen, given the pressure to resign? The prime minister, who won the most recent election by the biggest majority of his career, says he isn’t going anywhere.

In fact, seemingly oblivious to the mood on the street, he wants to further celebrate his stable of foreign architects. Near the end of my half-hour interview, he mentions that he is thinking of turning The Albanian Files into a museum, with one room for each of the 60 foreign firms to show drawings, models and photographs of their work. He said he already has a site for what he says will be “a big store of dreams, hopes and challenges, illusions and delusions. It will be a great place to go.” Its designer will be chosen, naturally, in an international architectural competition.

I left Albania with lots of questions and thousands of photos to sort through. In one of them I have my arm around Rama. Why the affectionate pose? A reflex when the camera comes out. But I suppose also that, politics aside, I can’t hate a man who loves architecture.

Prime Minster Rama with writer Fred Bernstein

The total market share for physical games was 11%, with free-to-play skewing it a bit

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new survey from research firm Newzoo, "supported" by G2A, found that physical games represent upwards of 11% of games played by dedicated gamers, or even 30% of console games owned.

"Physical formats remain part of this landscape, despite predictions of their demise," writes Newzoo research director Alexis Pamboris.

The report collected responses from just over 6,000 "premium gamers" spread evenly across six markets: the US, UK, Germany, Poland, Brazil, and Saudi Arabia. The fieldwork period defined is August 2026, which would notably be some time after Sony announced that it will end support for new physical games in January 2028.

The "premium gamer" group is defined as people "aged 18-55 who play on PC or a Gen 9 console at least monthly," and have played AA or AAA titles in the last year that were purchased or played through a paid subscription like PlayStation Plus or Xbox Game Pass. The average premium gamer, it turns out, plays about 13.3 games a year.

Among many other things, the report looks at how these games were accessed. 43% were owned, 19% were accessed via subscription, and 38% were free-to-play games.

In total, 11% of the games played by these 6,000 gamers were physical. However, if you only look at the 43% of owned games – that is, games people paid for individually – the physical component is just over 25.5%.

Console-only gamers reported 14% physical for total ownership against 32% digital ownership. Again looking purely at the ownership split and ignoring subscription and free-to-play, physical copies represent 30.4% of the paid-for console games played in this survey period.

Sign up to our Switch 2 Spotlight newsletter to make sure you get the very latest news on Nintendo's new console and its games every week

"While this seems high, many of them were older releases and some will have been bought on disc or as a code in a box. Cartridges also remain common on Nintendo Switch," the survey says of the 11% base physical figure.

The focus on games played and owned, not just new games purchased, also helps explain why PC-only gamers reported 6% physical ownership, which is likely explained by the enthusiast retro market. GOG may have something to say about that niche.

Physical ownership is also slightly higher than the base in specific markets. The US and UK clock in at 12% physical total – or 27.9% and 26.6%, respectively, looking only at physical and digital games owned.

Free-to-play games are the largest single market, to no one's surprise, averaging 38% across all markets and platforms.

"The older group is slightly likelier to have bought games than use a subscription, as well as play games from physical media," the report explains.

This leads nicely to why people said they buy physical games. Digital ownership is skewed toward convenience – instant access, a portable library, digital discounts, and digital-only games – but physical game buyers more equally specified a few reasons.

Reasons to buy physical games were "less nuanced" among people who owned less than 75% of their games digitally (again, that physical chunk brings us close to that 25 - 30% ownership figure). These reasons included (in descending order of weight):

Sony's decision to walk away from physical games stoked fierce divide across the industry. Creators like Hideo Kojima are "really sad" to see discs phased out, and Kenzo Saruhashi, head honcho at JRPG house NIS, reckons Sony is "hearing the feedback from customers" amid still-burning backlash.

Like many executives, original PlayStation creator Ken Kutaragi backs the company's decision to end discs, calling the backlash a "mentality issue" and arguing discs aren't "economically reasonable."

However, while it may immediately cut costs or increase per-unit profit, some analysts worry the end of discs may also push younger or casual players further into mobile, free-to-play, and PC, which are already eating a larger share of what used to be a very console-flavored pie.

PlayStation is ditching a $402 million market and destroying consumer "freedoms" by abandoning physical games, says major UK retail association

Forests planted to fight climate change may be fueling wildfires

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A global study reveals that planting forests can increase wildfire risk, threatening carbon storage and challenging climate strategies.

There’s something darkly ironic about planting a forest to store carbon and watching it go up in flames.

But new research suggests that planting trees can sometimes increase wildfire risk, threatening the very carbon those forests were meant to store.

A new global study reveals just how much that risk varies across different landscapes.

An international team of researchers from Spain, Chile, and Finland analyzed satellite records spanning more than two decades to track what happens when non-forested land gets converted into planted forests.

Their dataset was enormous: 67,248 patches across the world’s temperate regions, monitored from 2001 to 2023 using MODIS land-cover and fire records.

After accounting for climate, accessibility, and patch size, afforestation was associated with a 26% increase in fire occurrence per hectare per year and a 23% increase in expected burned area when fires did ignite.

“Forest plantations change the fuel conditions in which fires start and spread,” said lead author Irene Repeto-Deudero from the University of Cádiz and the University of Eastern Finland.

“We wanted to understand how these changes differ across landscapes, rather than assuming that tree planting has the same consequences everywhere.”

Those global averages, though, hide enormous variation. The researchers compared fire patterns across four major types of temperate landscapes.

Temperate conifer forests took the worst hit. Fire occurrence there surged 2.7-fold, and the expected burned area roughly doubled.

These landscapes already contain large amounts of vegetation and face increasingly extreme fire weather.

Adding dense plantations can create more continuous fuel, allowing fires to spread more easily.

Mediterranean forests, woodlands, and scrub saw a 38% jump in fire occurrence and a 9% rise in burned area. In absolute terms, the probability of fire per 100 hectares over a decade climbed from 7.8% to 11.0%.

These regions already contend with seasonal drought, flammable vegetation, and frequent human-caused ignitions.

Piling more biomass onto that tinderbox gives extreme fire weather more fuel to work with.

Temperate broadleaf and mixed forests told a stranger story. Fire occurrence actually dropped by 23% after afforestation – but expected burned area grew by 20%.

Fewer fires, in other words, but the ones that did ignite burned larger. That trade-off matters more than it might seem.

Nearly half of the world’s planted forests sit in this biome. Even modest shifts in fire behavior could carry substantial consequences.

Temperate grasslands, savannas, and shrublands were the one bright spot. There, fire occurrence fell 18% with no significant increase in burned area.

“These contrasting responses show why local ecological conditions must guide afforestation decisions,” explained study co-author Alejandro Miranda from the University of La Frontera.

“The same intervention can have very different consequences depending on the vegetation, climate, and existing fire regime.”

Geographically, the increases in wildfire activity clustered in regions that are already under fire stress.

These include western North America, central Chile, southern Europe, southwestern Australia, and parts of South Africa.

In these regions, expanding plantations and increasingly dangerous fire weather could combine to intensify wildfire risks.

The study also highlights a data point that deserves more attention. Plantations are roughly twice as likely to burn as native temperate forests.

And monoculture plantations – single-species stands planted in uniform rows – have been linked to particularly extreme fire behavior.

All of this creates a serious accounting problem for climate policy.

Afforestation has become a flagship mitigation strategy, with temperate regions identified as high-priority planting zones.

But wildfires don’t just destroy trees. They also release stored carbon back into the atmosphere, potentially reducing or even reversing the climate benefits of tree planting.

“These results therefore require that tree planting initiatives consider their exposure to fire,” said Susana Gómez-González from the University of Cádiz.

“As the climate warms, increased wildfire risk may impact the capacity of forest plantations to store carbon in the long term.”

Planted forests provide wood, protect soil, and deliver a range of ecosystem services. The point isn’t to stop – it’s to think harder about where to plant them.

“Plantations are necessary in many cases to produce wood and other ecosystem services, including soil protection,” said Blas Mola-Yudego from the University of Eastern Finland.

“However, their location must be carefully planned to avoid unintended consequences on local fire regimes.”

“Fire risk needs to be considered from the outset, alongside production goals and environmental benefits.”

The findings challenge the assumption that planting more trees automatically delivers greater climate benefits.

In some landscapes, the additional wildfire risk could undermine the carbon gains that plantations are meant to provide.

For policymakers counting on planted forests to help combat climate change, the message is clear: where trees are planted may matter just as much as how many are planted.

The study is published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

This Train Offers a Front-Row Seat to One of Colorado’s Greatest Natural Wonders

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“Locomotives all have their own attitudes and personalities,” says Adam Clawson, the 24-year-old engineer at the helm of Royal Gorge Route Railroad, as our train winds along the Arkansas River. “They are their own living, breathing machines, and you have to learn their personalities.”

It’s a warm Saturday afternoon in October, and Clawson is at the controls of a locomotive he knows well: “Black Betty,” a jet-black, 3,000-horsepower diesel-electric unit that runs along a 12-mile stretch of tracks in Southern Colorado. Clawson is a third-generation railroader, following in the footsteps of his grandfather and great-grandfather, who both worked along this same route.

The Royal Gorge Route Railroad winds along the Arkansas River in Colorado.

As he eases the rumbling locomotive through the gorge, he offers a running commentary on the geology and history unfolding outside the window. Millions of years ago, he says, as the Rockies rose above the plains, the Arkansas River began carving its ancient path through the emerging peaks. Over time, the water wore away the hard granite, cutting a narrow, V-shaped gorge that plunges as deep as 1,250 feet in places.

The result is the Royal Gorge, a geological wonder nicknamed the “Grand Canyon of the Arkansas River.” Visitors can experience the dramatic 10-mile-long chasm from several perspectives: From above, while gazing down from the Royal Gorge Bridge, the tallest suspension bridge in the nation (or for more adventurous travelers, while rock climbing a 5-hour via ferrata); and from below, while paddling the Arkansas’s roaring rapids on a whitewater raft. But for travelers looking to sit back and savor the scenery passing by, there's no better viewpoint than the Royal Gorge Route Railroad's open-air car, standing face-to-face with the staggering canyon walls.

The writer's view aboard the Royal Gorge Route Railroad

This train line is as historic as it is scenic. Though the tourist train didn't launch until 1998, the route itself has been here for nearly 150 years—but it was close to never being built in the first place. In the late 1870s, a pair of competing railroad companies—the Denver & Rio Grande and the Santa Fe—duked it out for the chance to build tracks through this narrow canyon. Their two-year battle, which involved everything from court injunctions to sabotage to gun-slinging hired guards, became known as the “Royal Gorge War.” Eventually, the two companies signed a treaty and the Denver & Rio Grande prevailed, building tracks that eventually reached the lucrative mining hub of Leadville.

Decades later, the train still threads through the same Old West landscape, but the ride is decidedly more comfortable. Today passengers can choose from three classes: Vista Dome, which offers nearly unobstructed views of the gorge from glass-enclosed dome cars; Deluxe Class in the vintage dining car, where four-top tables with cushioned booth-style seats and a bar are nestled against the large windows; and Coach Class, with four-person table seating and the option to order food and drink from the casual grill menu and bar. On select dates, there are also First Class dining tickets available for lunch and dinner, with elevated menus and wine pairings. Regardless of where you sit, all ticket holders have access to the open-air car, which is standing-room only. With Clawson or one of his fellow engineers at the controls, all travelers have to do is sit back and let the scenery unfold.

The journey begins roughly an hour south of Colorado Springs in Cañon City at a historic depot originally completed in 1914. A few miles later, a dilapidated pipeline comes into view, running along the canyon wall just above the river. Completed in 1910, this eight-mile-long, mostly wooden structure provided water to Cañon City until 1973. As the train passes under the Royal Gorge Bridge, Clawson encourages me to open the cab window, lean back, and look up. The wind whips my hair as I stare at the bridge some 1,000 feet overhead. It’s 18 feet wide, according to Clawson, but from this vantage point, it looks like a thin wire that’s been strung across a gaping expanse.

As the train works its way through the river gorge, water rushes past and red rock walls tower on both sides.

Next, the train squeezes through the gorge’s narrowest and deepest section, the towering red granite walls growing taller and more imposing as they tighten around the tracks. Then we chug over the famous “Hanging Bridge,” which was an engineering marvel when it was completed in 1879—and remains one even today. At this point, the chasm narrows to just 30 feet wide, creating a bottleneck that required a little ingenuity to get a train through. Engineer C. Shallor Smith came up with a brilliant solution: He designed a 175-foot-long bridge that’s suspended above the rushing water by A-shaped supports anchored to the canyon wall.

Soon after, Clawson spots a pair of plastic kayaks hauled out on a small beach on the other side of the waterway. “We might get mooned,” he warns, referencing how paddlers traditionally greet the train when it passes. But when we spot the kayakers standing on the bank a few minutes later, they simply smile and wave. Clawson blares the train’s horn in response.

Eventually, the train reaches its western terminus, the town of Parkdale. Clawson hits the brakes and we jump down onto the tracks. Standing in front of the train up close, I’m struck by just how imposing she is. Thinking back to Clawson’s personality comment, I imagine her as fiercely independent—a self-assured, no-nonsense type of gal who’s not easily pushed around.

We clamber back into the cab and the train heads in reverse, retracing the route toward Cañon City. I relax back into my seat, letting the engine’s vibrations and the rhythmic drone of the wheels lull me into a pleasant daze. Outside, the rocky surfaces of the gorge’s walls alternate between light and shadow while the river glints in the midday sun. High overhead, I spot a turkey vulture circling on a thermal, its wings arching in a shallow V as it glides without flapping.

Clawson follows my gaze. “You can’t make this up,” he says. “Somebody might type into AI, ‘Take this picture of me and make it look like I’m in paradise.’ [But] all I gotta do is step outside."

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

NYC Votes To Cut Construction Red Tape in Bid To Spur More Homebuilding

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

New York City voters overwhelmingly approved a plan to overhaul the city's notoriously complicated construction permitting process on Tuesday, handing Mayor Zohran Mamdani a victory in his effort to accelerate housing development.

Question 4, 1 of 5 ballot measures approved in the Nov. 3 election, received roughly 68% support, according to preliminary election results.

The measure promises to simplify construction by bringing 40 types of permits and approvals currently handled by 18 separate government offices and agencies into one centralized digital system.

"New Yorkers voted for a government that moves as fast as they do," Mamdani said in a statement on Tuesday night. The reforms would "directly address the affordability crisis and help New Yorkers stay in the city they love."

However, the new system isn't required to launch until 2030. Even when it does, faster permitting will address only one of the many obstacles making housing so difficult and expensive to build in New York.

"No single reform will be a silver bullet for housing supply and affordability, especially in a city like New York," explains Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com®. "Instead, each pro-supply policy change is a baby step in the right direction. And these marginal gains will add up."

The Big Apple needs approximately 700,000 additional homes over the next decade to meet existing and future demand, according to an August 2026 draft assessment from the city's housing and planning departments.

Yet developers filed plans for just 8,064 multifamily units in the second quarter of 2026, in a stunning 52% decline from the previous quarter, according to the Real Estate Board of New York.

That amounts to less than half the 17,500 new homes the city needs to add each quarter—and they're only proposed units, not completed homes.

Centralizing permits could help close that gap by cutting down the time and cost builders are forced to spend navigating city bureaucracy.

"Streamlining approvals can make a huge difference," Krimmel says. "It might not be the flashiest reform, but when it comes to new construction, time is money."

And permitting takes a very, very long time in New York City.

Of the nearly five years it takes to build a typical 20-unit apartment building in the Big Apple, roughly 1.5 years are spent securing permits alone, according to a September 2026 study examining construction timelines across 60 U.S. cities.

Now compare that to similar projects in Raleigh, NC, or Phoenix, where it takes roughly two years from application to completion.

"In a city like New York City with high land acquisition costs and a complex regulatory environment, predictability in timelines and financing can be critical for ensuring new construction can pencil," Krimmel says.

Research suggests those time savings could translate into more homes.

Cutting approval times by 25% could have increased multifamily housing production in Los Angeles by 12.7% between 2010 and 2022, according to a 2026 study published in the Journal of Urban Economics.

When researchers accounted for additional development that faster approvals might encourage, the estimated increase reached 23.7%.

The question for New Yorkers, though, is when those reforms might translate into more housing options and lower rents. The median monthly asking rent hit $3,785 in October—up 4.5% from a year ago.

Under the new rules, applicants would have one digital portal to submit documents, track applications, and obtain approvals, rather than navigating separate systems across multiple agencies.

But streamlining applications doesn't necessarily mean they'll be reviewed any faster.

In fact, the average time required to approve applications through the Department of Buildings' existing digital portal climbed from 8.3 days in 2020 to 20.3 days in 2025, according to a July report from New York State Comptroller Thomas DiNapoli.

And its hardly the only challenge. Krimmel says the overhaul won't address other barriers to construction, including zoning restrictions and the complexity of financing multifamily developments.

Still, he sees the reform as one step toward untangling a much larger problem.

"In the same way that a complex web of red tape and veto points is extremely good at creating barriers to new construction, real solutions will require the city untangles that web bit by bit," he says.

With the new system potentially four years away, the first test will be whether it actually shortens permitting timelines. Any corresponding increase in housing construction—and relief for renters—could take considerably longer.

As Mamdani acknowledged on Tuesday night, "Now comes the work of turning these votes into results."

She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

Kylie Kelce Reveals the One Thing That Makes Someone the Perfect Houseguest

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Kylie Kelce has revealed what a visitor needs to do to be the perfect guest in their home—and it has to do with impressing her and her NFL-star husband Jason Kelce's daughters.

On a recent episode of her podcast, "Not Gonna Lie," the mom of four revealed that their daughters— Finnley, 18 months, Bennett, 3, Elliotte, 5, and Wyatt, 7—love having guests read to them.

“I know one way to my girls’ hearts, and really kids, I always tell people you don’t need to bring gifts when you come to our house. If you come in, you sit down, you offer to read our girls a book, they will be like forever," she revealed.

Kelce added, “Like genuinely, we have obviously family [that] will come and they’ll bring this and they’ll bring that, but if when you come and you sit down, you read a book, I think it’s one of the best ways to connect with kids.”

The glimpse of her houseguest request came just days after she defended her and her spouse's decision to snap up multiple Philadelphia properties right next to each other.

In an episode of her podcast, which featured guest Nikki Glaser, Kelce, 34, and the comedian chatted about hate comments.

"Do you ever get in the comments? Do you ever get feisty?” Glaser asked.

In her reply, Kelce detailed an Instagram account that frequently posts information about their real estate.

“One of the ones that I did it—which was not to a person so it doesn’t really feel as aggressive—was like ‘Celeb Homes.’ They put a bird’s-eye view of where we live with our children, and they were trying to give details of our property," she said.

The account then revealed that the Kelce family had also purchased the home "next door."

“[They were like], ‘Why would you need this many houses?’ And I’m like, ‘for f---ing security and privacy, you pieces of s--t.' They left the post up, so it’s still there. You can still find it," Kelce explained.

“‘Why would anyone need three neighboring properties?’ For safety, privacy, and security. Get the f outta here," reads the comment Kelce left.

She then told the comedian, "I think I typed ‘get f---ed.’ And then when I tried to post it, it wouldn’t let me. Why? I want to tell people to get f---ed."

Her admission came after she offered a glimpse of the family's childcare arrangements during an appearance on an episode of "IMO with Michelle Obama and Craig Robinson." She explained that she and the former Philadelphia Eagles star prefer to keep their relatives closely involved in raising their children.

The couple have built a support system that allows them to balance their professional commitments with family life.

A major part of that arrangement involves Kelce's parents, Lisa and Ed McDevitt, who live just 15 minutes from the family's Philadelphia-area home.

Kelce explained that her parents regularly help care for the girls, making it possible for her and Jason to navigate their busy schedules without relying on round-the-clock childcare.

Although the couple do employ a nanny, Kelce revealed that the arrangement is limited to traditional working hours rather than having someone live in their home.

“My husband and I have very busy schedules. We don’t necessarily want a live-in nanny or someone who’s going to be raising our kids void of us. That feels weird. So we do a lot with my parents every chance we get. We do things with Jason’s parents. We find that those are the values that we were raised with that we want our kids to have, not just because we tell them that, but because we live it," she explained.

The decision, she explained, reflects their desire to remain actively involved in their daughters' everyday lives.

Rather than bringing in someone to provide full-time care, the Kelces turn to their extended family for additional support.

Kelce said maintaining those close family relationships is particularly important because she wants her daughters to grow up with the same values that shaped her and Jason's childhoods.

She also emphasized how grateful she is for the nanny who helps the family during the workday, acknowledging that the additional support is essential to managing their responsibilities.

However, the couple's approach to childcare goes beyond convenience.

For Kelce, living close to her parents has been a priority, and she admitted that the thought of settling somewhere far from her family has never appealed to her.

“We couldn’t do it without my parents. My parents live 15 minutes from us, and they have helped us in every way imaginable," she said.

The Pennsylvania native explained that remaining in the area where she grew up has allowed her to preserve those relationships while raising her own family.

That connection to her hometown has also influenced the kind of upbringing she hopes to provide her daughters.

Kelce wants the girls to become familiar with the places that shaped her childhood, from local businesses to the community organizations that continue to play a role in her life.

She believes that growing up with those personal connections can help children develop a stronger sense of responsibility toward the communities around them.

Her own involvement with the Eagles Autism Foundation is one example of how she has maintained those local ties.

For Kelce, the opportunity to contribute to the community where she was raised is another benefit of remaining close to home.

“Now, granted, we do have a nanny. I always give a shoutout because we couldn’t do it without her, but we have her during regular work hours and really nothing outside of that," she added.

Nintendo's main Switch cartridge manufacturer is leaving – that's unlikely to affect Switch 2 games

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Macronix is pivoting to supply AI data centers, but the company isn't making Switch 2 cartridges anyway

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Macronix, the manufacturer Nintendo relies on to make Switch 1 cartridges, is phasing out production of the chips that power those games. That certainly sounds like another blow to physical media, and it may well be, but as it stands this news is unlikely to affect cartridges for Switch 2.

Macronix reports record profits in its latest quarterly financial period, and according to chairman Miin Wu, it does not expect to be able to expand its manufacturing capacity in order to fuel further growth. "Instead, near-term growth will be driven primarily by price increases," Wu says, as reported by Taipei Times.

Switch cartridges are powered by flash memory, which is the same type of tech that AI data centers are now eagerly gobbling at massively inflated prices. Chip manufacturers have found that they can make a lot more money selling their goods to data centers rather than makers of consumer electronics, leading to the global memory shortages that have presented us with the threat of next-gen consoles costing $1,000 or more.

Wu notes that demand for the chips "used in data centers is very robust," which is why it's emphasizing those while phasing out production of ROM chips, which are the ones that power Switch game cartridges.

However, Macronix does not manufacture Switch 2 cartridges – at least as far as we know. Details on Nintendo's manufacturing partners are poorly reported, but teardowns suggest that Switch 2 cartridges are primarily made by a separate company called Rohm, as well as another, unidentified manufacturer.

As noted by Nintendo Patents Watch, Macronix had apparently been trying to enter the Switch 2 cartridge business, but has seemingly never completed the R&D work necessary to get the ball rolling. If Macronix had entered the space, it might've opened the door to cheaper or higher-capacity physical Switch 2 games, which may have swayed some publishers away from game-key cards.

But that's pure speculation. As it stands, Macronix's exit from the ROM business won't affect the Switch 2 cartridge market. The effect on Switch 1 games is more difficult to measure, since that market is shrinking anyway – Nintendo itself has no more Switch 1 games on the horizon, though boutique publishers continue to put out physical games for the system.

Sign up to our Switch 2 Spotlight newsletter to make sure you get the very latest news on Nintendo's new console and its games every week

Nintendo has recently reiterated that it has "no plans to ditch physical games," and it doesn't seem there's any reason to expect that to change with Macronix's pivot.

Nintendo "clearly revised" its messaging on physical media following PlayStation debacle, says ex-marketing lead

Dustin Bailey joined the GamesRadar team as a Staff Writer in May 2022, and is currently based in Missouri. He's been covering games (with occasional dalliances in the worlds of anime and pro wrestling) since 2015, first as a freelancer, then as a news writer at PCGamesN for nearly five years. His love for games was sparked somewhere between Metal Gear Solid 2 and Knights of the Old Republic, and these days you can usually find him splitting his entertainment time between retro gaming, the latest big action-adventure title, or a long haul in American Truck Simulator.

Andrea Bowers Salutes the Climate Change Activists of the 1980s at Fondazione Sandretto Re Rebaudengo

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Andrea Bowers’ solo exhibition “La Ragnatela” at the Fondazione Sandretto Re Rebaudengo in Turin, Italy, is unintentionally, aptly timed, given its focus on the politics of climate change: Last year, Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni hit pause on solar panels on farmland, calling it a “threat to our food sovereignty.”

Bowers’ show spotlights the Italian anti-nuclear feminist movement and women’s camp La Ragnatela—“the spiderweb”, in Italian—that launched in 1983 in Comiso, Sicily, to protest the installation of US nuclear cruise missiles at the NATO base in Comiso. The show is a mini-retrospective composed of Bowers’ political activist work which was in dialogue with the movement. There are works by Bowers that she created in 2003, straight out of graduate school, as well as pieces produced in 2013, 2022, 2023 and four newly created artworks for the exhibition. Related Articles Melting Ron DeSantis Statue Makes a Point About Climate Change in Florida Collective Climate Action Implemented by Los Angeles Arts Institutions

Much of La Ragnatela’s history has been forgotten, so Bowers worked with curator Bernardo Follini to find and rebuild the network of Italian ecofeminists. Follini found three women, who are now in their 80s and 90s, who contributed documents and clippings to the exhibition.

“The social engagement part of my practice is the least known about or documented because the art world is more focused on objects, but this is what I’m most proud of,” said Bowers during a preview in Turin, dressed in head-to-toe black with her Botticelli-esque hair flowing freely.

The show opens with Defense of Necessity (2003), a quilt that nods to women’s traditional crafts, and it’s one of the first pieces that Bowers created as a political artist. On close inspection of the quilt, there are sowings of spiderwebs, a symbol for the Women’s Pentagon Action, an American grassroots ecofeminist and anti-nuclear movement that was simultaneously taking place in Italy by way of La Ragnatela and in England with the Greenham Common Women’s Peace Encampment. Defense of Necessity is held up as a fence in the show. “The fence is the site often where nonviolence is questioned whether it’s becoming violence. Activists who are involved in nonviolence are always saying, if we cut that fence, does it become violence? Or is this called an offense of necessity, which is an argument that smaller injustices, or you break small laws to protect the world from larger injustices,” explained Bowers. “It’s a legal term and because it has the word ‘fence’ in it, it’s a play on words and it’s poetic.”

On the other side of the fence are four vitrine cases, two of which feature a timeline of the La Ragnatela movement. The others display letter exchanges between the women in Comiso and their comrades in England, who eventually traveled to Comiso to help them protest. There are photographs of some of the women who were arrested for protesting, along with a letter from one of the women, who was detained for a long time and put on trial, during which she wore a T-shirt that says “question authority.” Bowers took the tagline and added it to Neon Spiderweb #3 (2026), a neon spiderweb with the words sitting on the outer string of a web—the artist also made three other neon works with such slogans: “A better end of the world is possible,” “Return our future” and “To be free from fear.” The political slogans of hope pour over into Political Ribbons (2026), brightly colored sheets of silky ribbons that repeat the mottos on the neon signs or feature new sayings: “People over profits,” “Outlaw women are the only hope for earth’s survival” and “Widen the web to break the nuclear chain.” Visitors are urged to take ribbons from the wall with them when they visit the show. Bowers explains that it’s a “simple way for people to take a position.” It’s a methodology that she’s used before, adding that to this day, she still gets a thrill out of seeing them in the wild or online.

Bowers’ pieces in the show command attention no matter their size. Her graphite on paper works: Diabloblockade, Diablo Nuclear Power Plant, Abalone Alliance, 198 (2003) and Women’s Pentagon Action, 1981, Detail of Woven Web Around Pentagon (2003) are hyperrealistic. the artist has recreated photographs that were given to her by her mother’s friends who were part of the Californian eco-group Mother for Peace. In Cardboard (2026), a larger-than-life cardboard with an “empowered Italian woman” facing her back to the audience holding a flag that says “Sun and wind,” the artist taught herself how to use an iPad to draw the image that’s been projected onto a thick cardboard.

The show at the Fondazione Sandretto Re Rebaudengo is sort of a homecoming for Bowers, as she last showed at the foundation in 1998 as part of a group show titled “Guarene 98.” Some 28 years later, her pieces from the Chandeliers of Interconnectedness franchise grace the halls of the space as visitors make their way to other exhibitions by Nan Goldin or Joseph Yaeger. The show, however, feels cut short. Perhaps Bowers has more to say than can fit the space she was given. (It would have been a better idea to give her two galleries to really demonstrate two decades of hard passion for the environment.)

Still, she managed to make a mark with Neon Opland (2026), another neon sign that imitates a doodle from a La Ragnatela poster of a woman kicking a missile with the words “No ai missile” written beneath. It’s somewhat phallic, a comical touch made more apparent by teenage couple who were flirting on a bench opposite its neon lights.

Viktor Gyökeres the difference as Arsenal claim nervy win against Slavia Prague

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Another European away day, another victory for Arsenal: albeit only just. This time, Viktor Gyökeres stole the show as poor Slavia Prague matched the longest winless streak in European Cup and Champions League history despite a spirited late comeback. That unwanted record stands at 23, equalling the mark set by AEK Athens and FCSB – formerly known as Steaua Bucharest – while Arsenal are unbeaten in their 10 last away trips.

However, had it not been for a wonder save from David Raya, the Czech champions would have snatched a point after Wiktor Nowak pulled one back late on as Mikel Arteta’s usually watertight defence showed signs of fallibility under pressure.

In contrast to last week’s defeat against Fleetwood in the Carabao Cup, Arsenal had been cruising until the last 15 minutes after Noni Madueke fired them ahead early on, set up superbly by Gyökeres.

That was the Sweden striker’s third assist in his past five appearances, although Arteta will have been even more encouraged by the emphatic finish that appeared to put the game beyond Slavia’s reach just before the break.

But the Arsenal manager admitted his side had taken their eye off the ball by allowing their hosts back into the game with some uncharacteristic defensive lapses as Slavia racked up 18 shots to his side’s 12.

“At half-time, the message was clear: there is still a lot to do in this game,” said Arteta. “We were extremely far from our standards in everything that we have to do, in every basic, in every concept and in everything that we discussed to avoid the game that we played in the second half. That is a huge learning.”

Slavia’s solitary victory in this competition came in their first-ever match in 2007 and after losing 3-0 to Arsenal here exactly a year ago, they knew their chances of avoiding the unwanted milestone were extremely slim. Arteta’s side have developed a killer instinct on their travels in Europe – not since May 2025 in the second leg of their semi-final against Paris Saint-Germain have Arsenal lost an away game in 90 minutes.

After their exploits at Anfield on Sunday that took them three points clear at the top of the Premier League, Arteta heavily rotated his side again with six changes. It’s perhaps not surprising that Martín Zubimendi ran out of steam given the Spain World Cup winner made only his third start of a so far frustrating campaign.

For the third time in Arsenal’s past four matches, Gyökeres was entrusted with leading the line after finally getting off the mark with a scrappy strike against Fleetwood. He finished as top scorer last season with 21 goals but has clearly been struggling for form and confidence. Yet there was no doubting the quality of his assist for Madueke’s opening goal after just eight minutes.

Raya’s long ball should have been dealt with by the Slavia defence but they made the criminal error of ­allowing it to bounce. That was the cue for Gyökeres to come alive as he beat his marker to the ball, chested it down and played the perfect pass for the England forward to run on to and score. Arteta’s reaction on the touchline suggested he enjoyed it too.

Mikel Merino was Arsenal’s hero here last year with two goals and he could have extended their lead on three occasions before the half-hour mark. The lively Madueke created his best opportunity after cutting the ball back to Merino but he could not beat Nazar Domchak in Slavia’s goal.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

With Martin Ødegaard watching from the bench next to William Saliba on the latter’s return from a long-term back injury, Arsenal lacked some of their usual fluency in attack. But ­Slavia were still no match for them and their task became almost ­impossible seven minutes before the break. Gyökeres exchanged a smart one-two with Merino inside the area before curling a brilliant shot into the far corner.

To their credit, the hosts began the second half with much more aggression after introducing their record signing, Ange N’Guessan, from the bench. Ezri Konsa and Declan Rice both found themselves in the book as Arsenal were pinned back. David Jurasek let fly from long range and his effort was deflected just wide of Raya’s goal. From the resulting corner, Gabriel Magalhães somehow managed to clear two goalbound headers off the line in a matter of seconds. The Brazilian celebrated with a massive fist pump but his evening wasn’t over.

Mikulas Konecny should have done much better with another header before Raya managed to push Oskar Kubiak’s header on to the post despite being at full stretch in mid-air. Slavia eventually scored the goal all their endeavour deserved in the final minute of normal time when Nowak fired in at the far post. There were a few nervous moments in stoppage time as Konecny forced Raya into another save. Arteta breathed a sigh of relief at the full-time whistle as Slavia were condemned to their fate.

→ Baca artikel asli

Erling Haaland settles thriller for Manchester City to end RB Leipzig resistance

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Feed Erling Haaland and Manchester City’s totemic No 9 will score. The problem, recently, has been a paucity of openings for him.

This was true again before Elliot Anderson crossed and Haaland slid home a priceless winner for Enzo Maresca’s disjointed unit, Rayan Cherki hitting a post late on.

The win ends City’s sequence of two defeats in three games yet the manager has major issues. He has to plug a leaky defence and drill his midfield to exert control.

And there is a shaky Gianluigi Donnarumma: City’s goalkeeper was culpable for Ridle Baku’s first equaliser for Leipzig, as he was for Nicolas Jackson’s winner in the recent defeat at Aston Villa, plus two more in the Paris Saint-Germain loss last month.

The manager said: “It’s not a concern but it’s a mistake. But in 48 hours [Saturday] we have Nottingham Forest away, we need to be focused on that and then in the international break I will look. At the moment I’m not thinking about the goalkeeping situation. Gigio knows he was not good with the first goal.”

He was honest, too, about Antonio Nusa’s second for Leipzig, the forward taking advantage of a lax rearguard. “Both goals are quite silly goals we can avoid,” Maresca said.

Still, after going down against Villa and Brighton in the Premier League, the win is a certifiable tonic ahead of the Forest trip.

City’s quest to return to victorious ways featured an XI of four changes from Saturday’s Brighton reverse. Noteworthy was Maresca’s dropping of Rúben Dias as Abdukodir Khusanov’s pace was preferred at centre-back to the captain, Haaland taking the armband.

While the 2,000-plus travelling support were stationed in a quadrant to the right of Donnarumma’s goal, the noise came from the congregation behind Maarten Vandevoordt’s where a sea of red and white bounced to the tempo of a booming drum. They could roar particularly loud when Nusa flitted across City’s D and unloaded, and what should have been a regulation save for Donnarumma became a fumble – and corner for Leipzig.

As can be with this Maresca side, control was difficult to find and administer for any prolonged passage: Phil Foden twice lost the ball when turning into traffic was no aid, the second time meaning Nusa could zigzag towards goal.

The Brighton defeat featured close to zero service for Haaland and City’s 13-goal man was again hard to find, Matheus Nunes’s loose cross, when the captain lurked, emblematic. So, too, as a sign of Leipzig’s ascendancy, was Marc Guéhi scrambling to clear danger, Nusa firing in the ball from the left, and Christoph Baumgartner having space to shoot marginally wide of Donnarumma’s left post.

“Keep the ball and shift it faster” might have been Maresca’s instruction and when this happened City fashioned the breakthrough. Cherki speared a diagonal right-left into a galloping Nico O’Reilly, whose instant cross caused Castello Lukeba to panic and turn home, via a trailing left knee.

City had forced the issue and twice went close to a second moments later. First, O’Reilly floated the ball into Leipzig’s area but Haaland’s header faltered. Then the left-back was set up by Iliman Ndiaye’s cross only for O’Reilly to miss with the same part of his anatomy.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Yet as the second half began Leipzig were staring at a fifth defeat in the fixture, having won only once in six previous meetings, all in this competition.

Intent was signalled by Nusa’s mazy run along the left, his chip causing Donnarumma to push away when he might have caught. This roused the home supporters and better followed for their team – but not Donnarumma.

Anderson’s loose ball for O’Reilly near halfway was snaffled by Leipzig, who marauded down the right before Brajan Gruda fed an overlapping Baku. Now came Donnarumma’s folly because, while the right-back’s shot was true, it was steered along a very tight angle, yet City’s No 1 allowed it to go in between him and his left post.

Here was the latest goal-costing error from the Italian – one for Maresca to ponder. Yet 1-1 was still a fair result away from the Etihad Stadium so relief emanated from those in black when Ezechiel Banzuzi’s shot hit side-netting only. Donnarumma’s wobble continued, though, as he fumbled another Baku attempt, narrowly recovering.

City were about to show character and quality: Ndiaye’s pinpoint cross from the left had Enzo Fernández arriving to head in supremely and Maresca’s team led again. Not for long. Leipzig waltzed through with ease and with Nunes out of position Nusa finished expertly from the left.

After Anderson skied over from close-range, Haaland did what makes him so lethal: score with his sole chance. “Not sure how many touches I had, but most important is that I managed to help the team to win,” the Norwegian said.

Durbin Is Retiring, Marshall Just Lost—Why The Bill Threatening Your Credit Card Rewards Isn’t Dead

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Dick Durbin is retiring and Roger Marshall just lost his Senate seat. The two most prominent champions of the Credit Card Competition Act, the law intended to regulate credit card interchange which will mean far fewer rewards for consumers, will be gone in January. The bill threatening the economics of card rewards, and the airlines that depend on them, has support from President Trump and Vice President JD Vance was an original co-sponsor so it may come back with his presidential campaign. But the thing about this legislation is that it’s more useful as a way to raise campaign money from both retailers and banks than as something Congress actually passes. Departures of Durbin and Marshall haven’t made the Durbin-Marshall bill go away. Durbin identified Vermont Senator Peter Welch as his successor on the issue. The Airline Lobby Is Right To Worry In this week’s Airlines Confidential, Airlines for America CEO Chris Sununu warns that state initiatives interfering with card fees would eliminate points-funded travel. Banks buy miles because rewards help attract customers and their spending. By reducing margins on that spending, it’s less valuable to spend to encourage consumers to use the product. In markets where government has gotten involved in interchange rates we’ve seen: smaller initial bonuses higher annual fees annual caps on awards award chart devaluations (if each mile is worth less, an airline can still award one mile per dollar on spend despite receiving less money for it from the bank) It also means less revenue for the airline. In the third quarter, American Express represented all of Delta’s profit. American Airlines is barely breaking even even with several billion net from Citibank. We’d have much shakier airlines and fewer flights, and aspirational leisure destinations like Hawaii in particular would see less air service. What The Credit Card Competition Act Actually Does The current Senate bill, S. 3623, would require covered issuers with more than $100 billion in assets to enable at least two unaffiliated payment networks. At least one would have to be outside of Visa and Mastercard. Merchants get to choose the routing. That’s meant to put downward pressure on merchant payment costs by changing who controls which network handles the transaction. Cards issued in a three-party system, such as American Express issuing its own cards, are exempt from the routing requirement. Merchants benefit from accepting cards. They get more sales, and higher average sales. It’s often less expensive to accept credit cards than to take cash (due to employee theft, incorrect change, higher insurance costs). Retailers want the service cards offer, but want the government to force Visa and Mastercard to provide it for less. Trump’s promise of $1,200 in annual family savings was completely made up and inconsistent with the bill. Currently a majority of interchange gets rebated to the customer. Regulation of interchange has never lowered prices to consumers. And card payments, funded in large part by interchange, provide services to consumers beyond just payments (and borrowings). They often fund consumer protections – not just stronger dispute resolution than debit cards, but also extended warranty, return protection, and travel protections. The Bill’s Strongest Value Is As A Threat Back in July 2023, I argued that this was an especially useful issue for opening contribution wallets on both sides. Retailers have a large, concentrated interest in lowering their acceptance costs. Banks, payment networks and airlines have a large, concentrated interest in preserving the existing economics. An unresolved legislative threat gives both sides a reason to pour dollars into campaigns and political committees. I’d still bet on another round of proposals, threatened amendments and lobbying. Durbin and Marshall are leaving but the retailers and banks with billions at stake aren’t. Politicians still want the money. And card payments, funded in large part by interchange, provide services to consumers beyond just payments (and borrowings). They often fund consumer protections – not just stronger dispute resolution than debit cards, but also extended warranty, return protection, and travel protections.

Back in July 2023, I argued that this was an especially useful issue for opening contribution wallets on both sides. Retailers have a large, concentrated interest in lowering their acceptance costs. Banks, payment networks and airlines have a large, concentrated interest in preserving the existing economics. An unresolved legislative threat gives both sides a reason to pour dollars into campaigns and political committees. I’d still bet on another round of proposals, threatened amendments and lobbying. Durbin and Marshall are leaving but the retailers and banks with billions at stake aren’t. Politicians still want the money.
